# Decorative Endpaper

->**Decorative Geometric Pattern**<-

This page features a traditional decorative endpaper, likely used as a protective cover or a flyleaf for the manuscript. The design is characterized by a repeating geometric motif:

*   **Primary Motif:** Red lozenges (diamond shapes) arranged in a grid.
*   **Detailing:** Each diamond contains a central yellow dot.
*   **Background:** A vibrant yellow field densely populated with fine, red stippled dots.

<note>This type of printed paper was common in 19th and early 20th-century bookbinding. The presence of ink stains and surface wear indicates that this manuscript was a functional item, frequently handled by its owner, a retired priest from Jammu.</note>

# **Now Begins the <term>Tajika Neelakanthi</term>, accompanied by its Commentary and Illustrative Examples**

<note>original: "अथ ताजिकनीलकंठी सटीका सोदाहरणा प्रारभ्यते" (Atha Tājikanīlakaṇṭhī saṭīkā sodāharaṇā prārabhyate)</note>

<note>The word **Atha** marks a formal and auspicious beginning in Sanskrit literature. This specific text, the **Tajika Neelakanthi**, is a seminal 16th-century work on <term>Tajika Jyotisha</term>—a system of Indian astrology that integrated Persian and Arabic techniques, such as annual horoscopes (Solar Returns).</note>

<margin>The manuscript features a highly ornate nested decorative border in red and green ink. These borders serve not just as decoration but as a frame of reverence for the sacred and scientific knowledge contained within.</margin>

->**The Author and the System**<-

<note>The text was composed by **Neelakantha Daivagna**, a renowned scholar at the court of the Mughal Emperor Akbar. The "Tajika" system is famous for introducing unique planetary "aspects" and "yogas" (planetary combinations) that differ from standard ancient Indian astrology (Parashari), reflecting a vibrant era of cross-cultural intellectual exchange in India.</note>

**Tajika:** A branch of astrology focusing on annual predictions, influenced by West Asian traditions.
**Neelakantha:** The name of the author, also an epithet of the god Shiva.
**Commentary:** This version includes a *Tika* (explanation) to help the reader understand the dense poetic verses of the original.
**Illustrations:** The inclusion of *Udaharana* (examples) indicates that the manuscript provides practical case studies or sample charts to demonstrate the astrological rules.

<summary>This is the formal title page of the Tajika Neelakanthi, a classic 16th-century manual on annual astrology that blends Indian and Persian methods. It specifies that this copy includes both explanatory commentary and practical examples.</summary>

<keywords>Tajika Neelakanthi, Neelakantha Daivagna, Indian astrology, Jyotisha, Persian influence, annual horoscopes, Sanskrit manuscript, title page</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>The previous page was a blank flyleaf containing only modern archival notes. ->Collection of 81-year-old Dina Nath Raina, Jammu. Retired Priest.<-

# Salutation to the auspicious Ganesha. Salutation to the auspicious Saraswati. Salutation to the lotus feet of the auspicious Guru.

May the roaring <term>Heramba</term><note>A five-faced form of Ganesha, the elephant-headed god who removes obstacles.</note> grant prosperity to the universe! He who, out of playfulness, grasps his trunk—which resembles a staff—and circles it like a necklace, performing the frantic <term>Tandava</term><note>The divine dance of destruction and creation, usually associated with Shiva.</note> dance of <term>Pashupati</term><note>A title of Shiva, meaning 'Lord of Animals.'</note>. In the sky, he appears like a second solar disc, making the Mother of the World <note>original: "Jagadambika" (Parvati)</note> laugh with his unrestrained play. **(1)**

There was a scholar named **Divakara** <note>His name literally means 'The Sun.'</note>, who I believe was like the sun itself in the science of calculations. Through his own accurately composed works, he revealed the universal form of the science of the stars. **(2)**

He had five sons who were equal to one another. In the scriptures of calculation, they were like five moons; in debating with great opponents, they were like lions; and in the performance of sacrificial rituals, they were like the five sacred fires. **(3)**

Of them, the eldest was named **Krishna**. He was a knower of flawless speech and a man of virtuous conduct in the world. **(4)**

Born after him was the younger brother **Vishnu**, who attained the highest rank among the assembly of wise scholars. His fame was perpetually manifested through the science of light <note>original: "Jyotish-shastra" (astronomy/astrology)</note> by his students and their students. Known to kings for his merits, he was a leader among the Brahmins. His explanations of the texts humbled even the Guru of the Gods <note>Brihaspati</note>, and he stripped away the pride of those born into greatness. **(5)**

There was also **Mallari**, the leader of the calculators <note>astronomers</note> who made his lineage famous. He was an invincible knower of other scriptures and a great teacher. Like a second Lord Mallari <note>Shiva</note> who subdues the masses of enemies, he was a clear speaker in the world and a knower of all the fundamental astronomical systems <note>original: "Siddhanta"</note>. **(6)**

His younger brother was named **Keshava**, a moon to the ocean of joy for astronomers. Skilled in speech, he revived the world with the nectar of his words, a master of the fine arts. **(7)**

Now follows his younger brother, **Vishvanatha**, who through the grace of Lord Vishnu is a conqueror through merit alone. He is an expert in the play of the all-knowing astrologers, a "Lion among Men" <note>original: "Nrisimha"</note> who has mastered all branches of knowledge. **(8)**

There was **Nilakantha**, a knower of <term>Vedanta</term><note>The philosophical end-part of the Vedas.</note>, a leader in the codes of law and the six limbs of the Vedas, and a destroyer of the pride of others. He was the author of various works, a knower of the scriptures of the serpent-king <note>original: "Phanipati," referring to Patanjali's grammar</note>, and a resident of the great city of Kashi <note>Modern Varanasi</note>. This Nilakantha, devoted to the worship of the son of Shri Nilakantha, composed the **Sanjna-tantra**, which makes the difficult periods of planetary influence—both good and bad—easy to understand. **(9)**

**Vishvanatha**, the foremost of those who know planetary calculations, yet free from pride, resides in the village called **Gola** <note>Modern Golgaon in Maharashtra</note> on the banks of the Godavari river. For the perpetual benefit of the world, he has composed this new, flawless commentary in prose and verse on the *Sanjna-tantra*, to clarify its meaning and cut through the knots of difficulty for astrologers. **(10)**

To ensure the completion of the work without obstacles, Shri Nilakantha performs an auspicious invocation and declares his intent: He explains and makes clear the **Tajika** <note>A system of Indian astrology with Perso-Arabic influences.</note> through the well-spoken verses of the ancient teachers and through various meters. What kind of *Tajika*? One that "delights the minds of the learned." This means that by looking at this book, the hearts of scholars become joyful. Having done what? Having bowed to Heramba (Ganesha). And again, having...

->CC-0. Courtesy Sanjay Raina, Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri<-

<summary>This page contains the opening verses of the Sanjna-tantra, featuring a genealogy of the Golagrama family of astronomers (including Divakara, Krishna, and Vishnu) and introducing the author Nilakantha and the commentator Vishvanatha.</summary>
<keywords>Ganesha, Saraswati, Divakara, Nilakantha, Vishvanatha, Tajika, Sanjna-tantra, Golagrama, astrology, genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal technical text of the Tājika Nīlakaṇṭhī. It transitions from the family genealogy of the previous page into the standard opening invocations and the first descriptions of the zodiac signs, Aries and Taurus. This section belongs to the Sanjñā-tantra, the first part of the work focusing on definitions.</meta>

<margin>
TA. NI. <note>original: "Tā. Nī.", abbreviation for the title Tājika Nīlakaṇṭhī.</note>
|| 1 ||
</margin>

Having bowed to the Sun <note>original: "Divākara," which is also the name of the author's father.</note>, and further—what having done?—having bowed to the lotus feet of the Guru named **Anantadaivajña**. **(1)**

**The Male Sign** <note>Aries</note> is a masculine sign; **Movable**, meaning it has the nature of motion; **Fire**, being of the fire element; **Firm**, meaning it possesses a sturdy body; **Four-legged**, meaning it has four feet; **Red**, of a reddish hue; **Hot**, having a hot temperament; **Loud-voiced**, making a great sound; **Mountain-dweller**, roaming the peaks; **Fierce**, of a cruel nature; **Yellow**, also having a yellow color; **Strong in the day**, gaining power during daylight; **Lord of the East**, ruling the eastern direction; **Rising Unevenly** <note>original: "Viṣamodaya," referring to how the sign appears on the horizon.</note>; **Low Fertility**, meaning he has little contact with women and few offspring; **Rough**, lacking luster; **Royal**, belonging to the warrior caste <term>Kshatriya</term>; **Balanced**, having symmetrical limbs. Such is the **Ram** <note>Aries/Meṣa</note>; he is to be known by these specific qualities. This sign is described as being of two colors: reddish and yellow. For the signs of the Ram, the Lion, and the Archer...

<margin>
Preface
1
</margin>

> Having bowed to **Heramba** <note>Ganesha</note>, then to the **Sun**, and likewise to the lotus feet of the Guru **Ananta**, the illustrious **Nilakantha** clarifies the **Tajika** system <term>Tājika</term><note>A branch of Indian astrology heavily influenced by Persian and Arabic traditions.</note> through eloquent sayings to delight the minds of the scholars. **(1)**
>
> Now, he describes the nature of the twelve zodiac signs. First, the **Ram** [Aries]:
>
> Masculine, movable, fire, firm, four-legged, red, hot, bilious, loud-voiced, mountain-dweller, fierce, yellow, strong in the day, ruling the East, rising unevenly, low fertility and sexual union, rough, royal, balanced—this is the Ram. **(2)**
>
> Now, he describes the nature of the **Bull** [Taurus]:
>
> The Bull is fixed, female, earth, cold, dry, lord of the South, dweller of good lands, windy, strong at night, four-legged, white, loud-voiced, rising unevenly, moderate offspring and sexual union, auspicious, and of the merchant caste. **(3)**

<margin>
|| 1 ||
</margin>

...for the Ram [Aries], the Lion [Leo], and the Archer [Sagittarius], these two colors are mentioned. Why? Because it was stated so by **Samarasimha** <note>An earlier 13th-century authority on Tajika astrology.</note>. **(2)**

**The Bull** [Taurus] is **Fixed**, meaning it is of a stable nature; **Female**, a feminine sign; **Earth**, an earthy sign; **Cold**, of a chilly temperament; **Dry**, lacking luster; **Lord of the South**, ruling the southern direction; **Dweller of Good Land**, moving in pleasant territories; **Windy**, of a flatulent or airy constitution; **Strong at Night**, gaining power during the dark hours; **Four-legged**, having four feet; **White**, of a bright color; **Loud-voiced**, making a great sound; **Rising Unevenly**; **Moderate Fertility**, having a middling number of children and moderate sexual contact; **Auspicious**, being of a gentle nature; **Merchant**, belonging to the <term>Vaishya</term> caste. This sign also has beautiful limbs. The "firm-limbed" quality was mentioned only for the Ram, the Lion, and the Archer; by implication, the others are to be understood as having different bodily proportions. **(3)**

<summary>This page introduces the Tājika Nīlakaṇṭhī with invocations to Ganesha and the author's teacher, followed by a detailed classification of the first two zodiac signs, Aries (Meṣa) and Taurus (Vṛṣa).</summary>
<keywords>Nilakantha, Tajika, Aries, Taurus, Zodiac attributes, Samarasimha, Anantadaivajña, Indian Astrology, Meṣa, Vṛṣa</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the zodiac signs (Rashis) within the Sanjñā-tantra section of the Tājika Nīlakaṇṭhī. Having previously described Aries (Mesha) and Taurus (Vrishabha), the text now details the physical and temperament qualities of Gemini, Cancer, and Leo.</meta>

**The Pair** <note>original: "Dvandvam"</note> is to be known as the sign of **Gemini**; its characteristics are as follows: it is the ruler of the **West**, and is a **Wind** sign <note>The element of Air</note>. It has the **luster of a parrot**, meaning its color is green. It is **two-footed**, meaning it has two legs <note>human-like</note>, and is a masculine sign. It is **dual-bodied**, meaning it possesses a **dual nature** <term>Dvishvabhāva; having both movable and fixed qualities</term>. In this regard, a specific distinction <unclear>pa</unclear> should be known: the first half of the sign is fixed and the second half is movable. This was stated by **Bhattotpala** <note>A famous 10th-century commentator on astrological texts</note>: "In a dual-natured sign, the results are mixed or opposite; in the first half it is like a fixed sign, and in the latter half it is like a movable sign." It **rises unevenly** <note>original: "vishama-udaya"</note>, is **hot** by nature, and possesses **medium fertility**, meaning a moderate number of children and moderate contact with women. It is a **forest-dweller**, a roamer of the wilds, and belongs to the **Shudra** <term>the laborer or artisan caste</term>. It has a **long voice**, meaning it makes a great sound, and is **oily** <note>original: "snigdha," often meaning a smooth or healthy complexion</note>.

### Now he states the form of the sign Gemini:
West, Wind, parrot-hued, two-footed, the pair, dual-bodied, rising unevenly, hot; medium offspring and contact with women, forest-dwelling, Shudra, long-voiced, oily, and strong during the day. **(4)**

### Now he states the form of the sign Cancer:
High fertility and many feet, the Crab is movable, female, pale red, and soft-voiced; auspicious, phlegmatic, oily, a water-dweller, rising evenly, a Brahmin, and strong at night in the North. **(5)**

### Now he states the form of the sign Leo:
Male, fixed, fire, day-strong, yellow, rough; bilious, hot, ruler of the East, firm, four-legged; rising evenly, loud-voiced, small offspring, the Lion, king of the mountains, fierce, and smoky-hued. **(6)**

---

**Smooth** <note>original: "chikkaṇa"</note>, **strong during the day**, and the word "dattayora" in the verse is used to complete the meter. **Fierce** means cruel; this refers to the limbs of the sign. **(4)**

**The Crab** <note>original: "Kulira"</note> is the sign of **Cancer**; it is to be known as follows: **High fertility**, meaning many children and much contact with women; **many feet**, meaning many legs <note>as a crab has</note>; **movable**, meaning it belongs to the movable category of signs. It is a **female** sign, **pale red** <note>original: "pāṭala," a pinkish-red like a trumpet flower</note> in color, and **soft-voiced**, meaning quiet. It is **auspicious** or gentle, and **phlegmatic** <term>Kapha; one of the three humors in Indian medicine</term> by nature. It is **oily** or smooth, a **water sign**, and a **water-dweller**. It **rises evenly**, belongs to the **Brahmin** <term>the priestly or scholarly caste</term>, is **strong at night**, and is the **lord of the North**, ruling that direction. This refers to its physical form. **(5)**

**The Lion** <note>original: "Hari"</note> is **Leo**; it is to be known as such: **Male**, **fixed** <term>Sthira; stable and unchanging</term>, and a **fire sign**. It is **strong during the day**, **yellow** in color, and **rough**, meaning lacking in luster. It is **bilious** <term>Pitta; the fire-based humor</term> and **hot** by nature. It is the **lord of the East**, ruling that direction, and is **firm**...

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the zodiac signs (Rashis) from the Tajika Nilakanthi, a classical text on Persian-influenced Sanskrit astrology. The previous page concluded with the beginning of the description for Leo. This page completes Leo and provides the full characteristics for Virgo (Kanya), Libra (Tula), and Scorpio (Vrishchika).</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>
<sig>Ta. Ni. || 2 ||</sig>
<header>Collection of 81 Year Old Dina Nath Raina, Jammu. Retired Priest.</header>

...strong-bodied, four-footed <note>a quadruped</note>, and rises evenly. It has a **long voice**, meaning it makes a great sound. It has **little contact and progeny**, meaning infrequent sexual union and few children. It is a **dweller of rocks and mountains**, and is a kingly sign of the **Kshatriya** <term>warrior or ruling caste</term>. It is fierce, cruel, and **smoke-colored**. This sign has two colors: yellow and smoky. **(6)** <note>This concludes the description of Leo (Simha) from the previous page.</note>

The nature of **Virgo** is as follows: it is pale or tawny-colored, **two-footed** <note>human-like</note>, and a feminine sign. It is **dual-bodied**, meaning it has a dual nature <term>Dvishvabhava; a mix of fixed and movable qualities</term>. It is the ruler of the **South** (the direction of Yama), is strong at night, and has a **windy** nature. It is cold in temperament, rises evenly, and is an **Earth sign**. It is "half-voiced" or "broken-voiced," and its domain is auspicious ground.

### Now he states the form of the sign Virgo:
->Pale, two-footed, feminine, dual-bodied, ruler of the South, night-strong, windy, cold, rising evenly, and Earth-natured. Virgo is half-voiced, on auspicious ground, of the Vaishya<-
->caste, dry-skinned, with little contact or offspring, and is auspicious. **(7)**<-

### Now he states the form of the sign Libra:
->Masculine, movable, multi-colored, rising evenly, and hot; ruler of the West, windy, oily, soundless, and a forest-dweller. Of few progeny and little<-
->sexual contact, of the Shudra caste, fierce; Libra is strong by day, two-footed, and balanced. **(8)**<-

### Now he states the form of the sign Scorpio:
->Fixed, white, feminine, water-element, ruler of the North, night-strong, soundless, many-footed, and phlegmatic.<-
->Rising evenly, water-dwelling, having excessive contact and progeny, auspicious, oily-bodied, of the Brahmin caste—this is the Scorpion. **(9)**<-

The commentary explains: **Vaishya** <term>the merchant or farming caste</term>. **Dry-skinned** means lacking luster. **Little contact and offspring** means infrequent union and few children. It is an auspicious and gentle sign; the word "and" <note>original: "cha"</note> is used here in a cumulative sense. **(7)** **Masculine** means it is a male sign. **Movable** <note>original: "chara"</note> is its technical classification. **Multi-colored** means it possesses various hues. It rises evenly and is hot. It is the ruler of the **West**, and is a **Wind** sign. It is **oily**, meaning it has a smooth or glossy quality. **Soundless** <note>original: "ravono"</note> means it makes no noise. It is a **forest-dweller**. **Few progeny and contact** means small numbers of children and infrequent sexual union. It belongs to the **Shudra** <term>the laborer or artisan caste</term>. It is fierce, strong during the day, two-footed, and **balanced**, meaning its limbs are symmetrical. Such is the sign of **Libra**. **(8)** **Fixed** is its classification. **White** is its color. It is a feminine, **Water** sign. It is the ruler of the **North**, and is strong at night. **Soundless** means it makes no noise. It is **many-footed** <note>referring to the many legs of a scorpion</note> and is dominated by **phlegm** <term>Kapha; one of the three humors in Indian medicine</term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the zodiac signs (Rashis) from the Tajika Nilakanthi. The previous page ended with the description of Libra and the start of Scorpio. This page concludes Scorpio and provides full characteristics for Sagittarius (Dhanu), Capricorn (Makara), and Aquarius (Kumbha).</meta>

<header>Collection of 81 Year Old Dina Nath Raina, Jammu. Retired Priest.</header>

...of a **phlegmatic nature** <term>Kapha: the bodily humor associated with water and earth</term>, even-rising, and a water-dweller. It is highly prolific in offspring, meaning it is associated with many wives and many children. It is auspicious, gentle, and has a smooth or oily body. It is of the **Brahmin** <term>priestly or scholarly caste</term> caste. Such are the qualities to be known for the sign of the Scorpion <note>original: "Alivrishchika"</note>; this concludes the verse. **(9)**

### Now he states the form of the sign Sagittarius:
->Masculine, golden-hued, a mountain-dweller, even-rising, and very loud. It is strong during the day and rules the **East**. It is firm-bodied, dry <note>original: "ruksha"; lacking in oiliness or luster</note>, and yellow in color. It is a kingly sign of the **Kshatriya** <term>warrior or ruling caste</term> caste. It is of a hot, bilious nature <term>Pitta</term>. It is said to have little progeny and infrequent sexual union. It is dual-bodied, meaning it possesses both movable and fixed natures. It is two-footed, though specifically, it is two-footed in its first half and four-footed <note>quadruped</note> in its second half—as will be explained further. It is a fire sign, fierce and cruel.<-

->The sign of **Sagittarius** <note>original: "Dhanu"</note> is masculine, golden, mountain-dwelling, even-rising, and loud-voiced. It is day-strong, rules the East, and is firm, dry, and yellow. It is a kingly, hot, bilious sign with little offspring, dual-bodied, two-footed, and a fierce fire sign. **(10)**<-

### Now he states the form of the sign Capricorn:
->**Capricorn** <note>original: "Mriga" or "Makara"</note> is movable, an Earth sign, and half-voiced. It rules the **South** <note>original: "Yamasha"; the direction of Yama, lord of death</note>. It is a feminine sign, tawny and dry. It dwells on auspicious ground and is cold in nature. It has little progeny and infrequent sexual union. It is of a windy nature <term>Vata</term> and is strong at night. In its first half, it is four-footed; in its second half, it is a water-dweller—this will be explained later. It is uneven-rising and of the **Vaishya** <term>merchant or agriculturist caste</term> caste. This concludes the verse. **(11)**<-

### Now he states the form of the sign Aquarius:
->**Aquarius** <note>original: "Kumbha"</note> is footless <note>original: "apada"; likely referring to the image of the water pot</note>, masculine, and strong during the middle of the day. It has moderate progeny. It is a **fixed** sign, variegated in color, and a forest-dweller. It is a wind sign. It is smooth, hot, and has a broken or "cut" voice. It is of the **Shudra** <term>laborer or artisan caste</term> caste, rules the **West**, and is uneven-rising. **(12)**<-

This sign Sagittarius is to be known as having two colors: gold and yellow. **(10)** The sign Capricorn <note>original: "Mriga"</note> is to be known thus: it is "movable" by name. It is an Earth sign with "half-sound" or moderate voice. It rules the Southern direction. It is a feminine sign, tawny-colored, and dry (lacking luster). It is auspicious, gentle, a dweller of the earth, and cold in temperament. It has little offspring and infrequent union. It is of a windy nature and strong at night. In the first half of the sign, it is understood to be four-footed, while the second half is a water-dweller, as will be noted later. It rises unevenly and is of the Vaishya caste. This concludes the verse. **(11)** The sign Aquarius is to be known thus: it is footless, a masculine sign, and strong during the day...

...prolific union, with moderate sexual union and moderate offspring. ### Now he states the form of the sign Pisces:

->**Pisces is footless, a feminine sign, of a phlegmatic nature, a water sign, and strong at night. It is silent, brown-colored, dual-bodied, and dwells in water. It is smooth, has excessive sexual union and offspring, belongs to the Brahmin caste, is auspicious, rules the North, and is odd-rising. (13)**<-

<margin>Ta. Ni. Pra. 1</margin>
The sign of **Pisces** <note>original: "Meena"</note> is to be known as follows: it is **footless** (meaning without feet or legs); a **feminine sign**; **phlegmatic** (of a Kapha nature); a **water sign**; **night-strong** (possessing strength during the hours of darkness); **silent** (meaning without sound); **brown** (tawny or yellowish-brown in color); **dual-bodied** (possessing both movable and fixed qualities); and **water-dwelling**. It is **smooth** (or oily) and **highly prolific** (meaning it is associated with frequent sexual union and many offspring—the word "api" here implies "furthermore"). It is of the **Brahmin** <term>priestly or scholarly caste</term> caste, **auspicious** (gentle), and rules the **North**. It is **odd-rising** and long-bodied.

### Having thus described the forms of the signs, he now states the division of friendship and enmity among the signs:

->**Friendship exists between the groups of Earth and Water signs, and between the groups of Fire and Air signs. Otherwise, there is enmity. The latter half of Sagittarius is known to be four-footed, while the latter half of Capricorn is aquatic. (14)**<-

...odd-rising and long-bodied. **Friendship** <note>original: "suhrittvam"</note> is found in the group consisting of **Earth signs** and **Water signs**. Similarly, friendship is to be known between the group of **Fire signs** and **Air signs**. Otherwise (in cases contrary to this), there is **enmity** <note>original: "aribhava"</note>. **(13)** 

The meaning is this: when considering the friendship between a father and son, a husband and wife, a master and servant, or any others, one should declare friendship if the relationship involves an Earth sign and a Water sign. Likewise, friendship should be declared in the relationship between Fire signs and Air signs. Otherwise, there is enmity—such as between Earth and Fire, Earth and Air, Water and Fire, or Water and Air. Furthermore, if two people belong to the same group (for instance, both are Earth signs), then it is established by the sense of the text that there is great mutual affection between them.

Now, because certain specific characteristics were not mentioned during the previous descriptions of **Sagittarius** and **Capricorn**, he states them here using the **"Lion's Gaze" method** <term>Simhavalokana-nyaya: a logical principle where one looks back at what was previously stated while moving forward, much like a lion glancing backward while walking</term>. For **Sagittarius** <note>original: "Chapa" or "Dhanu"</note>, in its final portion (the second half), it is to be known as **four-footed** <note>quadruped</note>. For **Capricorn** <note>original: "Mriga" or "Makara"</note>, in its final portion (the second half), it is to be known as **aquatic** <note>living in water</note>. **(14)**

"Bile and wind," etc. **Aries** <note>original: "Mesha", appearing here as "Mepa" due to regional scribal habits</note> is **bile** <term>Pitta: the fiery humor associated with digestion and heat</term>; **Taurus** <note>original: "Vrisha", here as "Vrupa"</note> is **wind** <term>Anila or Vata: the airy humor associated with movement</term>; **Gemini** <note>original: "Mithuna"</note> is **balanced in humors** <term>Dhatusama: a state where wind, bile, and phlegm are equal</term>; and **Cancer** <note>original: "Karka"</note> is **phlegmatic** <term>Kapha: the watery humor associated with stability and lubrication</term>.

Starting from Aries and ending with Pisces, these humors repeat in three cycles of four. These signs should be known and understood by the **learned scholars** <note>original: "Suribhi" or "Panditai"</note>. 

Now, one must understand the three cycles of the four **castes** <term>Varnas</term>—the **Ruler** <note>original: "Rajanya" or Kshatriya</note>, the **Merchant** <note>original: "Vit" or Vaishya</note>, the **Laborer** <note>original: "Shudra"</note>, and the **Priest** <note>original: "Dharasura" or Brahmin</note>—among the twelve signs. 

Specifically: 
* **Aries** is a Ruler (Kshatriya).
* **Taurus** is a Merchant (Vaishya).
* **Gemini** is a Laborer (Shudra).
* **Cancer** is a Priest (Brahmin).

Likewise, Leo is a Ruler, Virgo is a Merchant, Libra is a Laborer, and Scorpio is a Priest. Similarly, Sagittarius is a Ruler, Capricorn is a Merchant, Aquarius is a Laborer, and Pisces is a Priest. All astrological results for these signs should be determined according to these natures.

### Now, to describe the results of the aforementioned forms of the signs, he states their nature in brief:

->**Bile, wind, balanced humors, and phlegm occur in groups of three starting from Aries; this is to be understood by the learned. The Ruler, Merchant, Laborer, and Priest castes also cycle through, and all results shall be according to the sign. (15)**<-

### Now, he describes the method for finding the "commencement of the year" to calculate the yearly horoscope:

->**The elapsed years, increased by one-fourth of their value, are multiplied by "Nature" [21]. Add forty [40] ghatikas and the weekday of birth. The weekday of the year's entry is found by dividing the total by seven. (16)**<-

The meaning is that the results will be similar to the inherent form of the sign. Now, he will discuss the mathematics. First, he will describe the "movement of the planets" <term>Graha-chalana</term>. For that movement, the "commencement of the year" <term>Varshapravesha: the exact moment the Sun returns to its birth position</term> is required. The author has not stated it here because it was already mentioned in the *Varshatantra* <note>The 'Varshatantra' is the specific section of Nilakantha's work dealing with annual techniques</note>. However, we are presenting it here because it is necessary for our use. 

Furthermore, the determination of the **Year Lord** <term>Varsha-esha</term>, the major and minor planetary periods <term>Dasha-Antardasha</term>, and the commencement of months and days are also useful here. These were shown in the *Varshatantra*, so they are not repeated here in detail; however, we will provide the explanation wherever it is needed. **(15)**

"Elapsed years" <note>original: "Gatah samah"</note> refers to the years that have passed since birth. To find this: the **Era** <term>Shaka</term> at the time of birth should be subtracted from the current year's Era; the remainder is the "elapsed years." These should be increased by their fourth part <note>This "quarter-addition" is a mathematical adjustment for leap years in the solar calendar</note>. "Nature" <note>original: "Prakriti"</note> refers to the number twenty-one; the group of elapsed years is multiplied by that. From that, "forty" <note>original: "Khaveda"; in the Sanskrit numerical code, Kha=0 and Veda=4, read right-to-left as 40</note> along with the *ghatikas* <term>Ghatika: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> are added. These are then added to the weekday of birth...

</margin>

...the days, **ghatis** <term>Ghati: a traditional Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>, and **palas** <term>Pala: a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</term> of the birth time should be added. Then, divide the topmost figure by seven; the remainder indicates the day of the week. In this book, one should declare the "Year Entrance" <note>original: "Abda-pravesha"</note> or "Annual Return" based on the resulting weekday, ghati, and pala. This should be known as the current weekday of the return. ॥ 16 ॥ 

The "Years Elapsed" <note>original: "Abda," referring to the number of years since birth</note> should be multiplied by eleven <note>original: "Shivaghna," where Shiva represents the number 11</note>, then increased by its own one hundred and seventieth part <note>original: "Svakhadrimdulavadhya": Sva (self) + Kha (0) + Adri (7) + Indu (1)</note>. To this, add the lunar day of birth <note>original: "Janma-tithi"</note>, counting from the first day of the waxing moon. Then, subtract or divide by thirty <note>original: "Svagnishodhita": Agni represents the number 3, here meaning 30</note>. The remainder indicates the lunar day on which the Year Entrance occurs. There is no absolute rule that the Year Entrance must fall on this exact lunar day; sometimes it occurs on the preceding or following lunar day. In this matter, the calculated **weekday** is the true authority. Among the three possible lunar days, the one that coincides with the previously calculated weekday is to be known as the true date of the Year Entrance. Where a remainder is found after division, the quotient is discarded and only the remainder is taken by the **learned scholars**. ॥ Now the sequence for a practical example is written. First:

### Now the method for determining the Lunar Day (Tithi) is stated: The years elapsed, multiplied by eleven, increased by their one hundred and seventieth part, and reduced by thirty, then added to the birth lunar day; on that lunar day, the Year Entrance occurs. ॥ 17 ॥

<margin>

</margin>

An example of a birth is now written: In the year **1648 of the Vikrami Era** <note>Samvat 1648, approx. 1591 CE</note>, **1513 of the Shaka Era**, in the month of Magha, during the waxing fortnight, on the 1st lunar day, a Wednesday. The time was 36 ghatis into the day, under the Shravana star (nakshatra) which lasted 54 ghatis, with the Vajra yoga lasting 6 ghatis. On this auspicious day, the time elapsed from sunrise was 37 ghatis and 53 palas. The elapsed night time was 12 ghatis and 31 palas. The local latitude <note>Akshama</note> was 7;30 and the longitudinal correction <note>Deshantara</note> was 30. The Sun’s position was 9 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds. The rising sign <note>Lagna</note> at birth was Capricorn at 5 degrees, 10 minutes, 53 seconds, and 50 thirds. 

Now, the example for the Year Entrance: The Shaka year at birth was 1513. Subtracting this from the current Shaka year of 1550 <note>approx. 1628 CE</note>, the remainder is 37, which is the number of elapsed years. This is increased by its fourth part (9;15;0), making the total 46. The 37 elapsed years are also multiplied by twenty-one, resulting in 777. This is divided by forty, giving a result in ghatis and palas of 19;25;30. Adding this to the previous calculation of 46;15;50 results in 46 ghatis, 34 palas, 25 vipalas, and 30 thirds. When added to the birth weekday and time (4 days, 37 ghatis, 52 palas), the sum is 51;12;18;30. Dividing the total days by seven, the remainder is **2** <note>Monday</note>, at 12 ghatis, 18 palas, and 30 vipalas—this is the moment of the Year Entrance. 

Now, he states another method for bringing about the Year Entrance: The constant for the year <note>Varsha-dhruvanka</note> is 36;15;13;30. This is multiplied by the 37 elapsed years, resulting in 135;14;34;25;30. To this, add the birth weekday and time of 4;37;52...

<meta>This page continues the technical calculation of the "Year Entrance" (Solar Return), moving from the general formula for determining the weekday to a specific example involving a 37-year-old individual, and then transitioning into a scholarly defense of precise solar positioning against earlier methods.</meta>

The sum results in this number: 1351912 | 18 | 30. Dividing the top figure by seven, the remainder indicates the weekday and time of the Year Entrance: 2 | 12 | 18 | 30 <note>In this system, 2 represents Monday, starting the count from Sunday.</note>.

### Calculation of the Lunar Day (Tithi)

Now, the calculation of the lunar day: The number of elapsed years is 37. Multiplied by eleven, it becomes 407. This is increased by its own 170th part, which is 2 <note>407 divided by 170 is approximately 2.39; here, the integer 2 is added</note>, totaling 409. To this, add the 2 lunar days elapsed from the first day of the waxing moon at the time of birth, totaling 411. When divided by thirty, the remainder is 21. This indicates that the Year Entrance occurs on the 6th day of the dark fortnight <term>Krishna Paksha Shashti: the 21st lunar day of the month</term>.

Because the previously calculated weekday does not fall on this lunar day, but rather on the following one, we add 1 to the lunar day: 21 + 1 = 22. Thus, the date is the 7th day of the dark fortnight <note>Krishna Paksha Saptami</note>.

In whatever month the birth occurred, the Year Entrance usually occurs in that same month. Occasionally, it may occur in the preceding or following month. The proof for this is as follows: The month in which the Sun's position is exactly equal to the Sun's position at the moment of birth is to be known as the month of the Year Entrance.

**The Calculated Date:**
**Vikrama Samvat 1785; Shaka 1650; Month of Magha, 7th day of the dark fortnight; Monday.**
**Time:** 37 **ghatis** <term>Ghati: a unit of 24 minutes</term> and 39 **palas** <term>Pala: a unit of 24 seconds</term>.
**Nakshatra** <note>Lunar mansion</note>: Hasta (17 ghatis, 28 palas).
**Yoga** <note>Auspicious period</note>: Sukarma (27 ghatis, 28 palas).
On this auspicious day, the Year Entrance for the 38th year occurs at 12 ghatis, 18 palas, and 30 vipalas after sunrise.

### On the Authority of the Year Entrance Time

Now, the authority for this timing of the Year Entrance is stated:
"The time of the Year Entrance is determined by the Sun. The wise must certainly know that the month of Year Entrance is when the Sun’s position is exactly equal to the Sun’s position at the moment of birth. Because of this, the Sun calculated through the method of Year Entrance becomes equal in signs, degrees, minutes, and seconds to the birth-Sun. This is the very proof of the Year Entrance time."

An example of this will be shown further on. Ganesha Daivajna <note>A famous 16th-century Indian astronomer</note>, in his own work *Tajika Bhushana*, realized that the Sun at the calculated Year Entrance did not perfectly match the birth-Sun’s minutes. To resolve this "confusion" regarding the timing of the Year Entrance, he suggested an alternative refinement:

1. Subtract the birth Shaka year from the current Shaka year. Multiply the remainder by 5, then divide by 2164. The result, when added to the weekday and ghatis of birth and divided by seven, certainly gives the Year Entrance. ॥ 1 ॥

2. At the time of the Year Entrance, measured in ghatis and palas, the Sun is indeed most accurate. This should be calculated according to the Siddhantic <note>canonical astronomical</note> rules so that it is equal to the birth-Sun. ॥ 2 ॥

3. If the Sun, which delights the lotus-like day, does not match exactly in its minutes, then one should adjust the difference based on the speed of the Sun. ॥ 3 ॥

4. From this, the Year Entrance becomes accurate. One should then determine the fruits of the year based on the planetary houses and positions existing at that specific moment. ॥ 4 ॥

Now, an example is shown to remove the confusion caused by Ganesha Daivajna...

<meta>This page continues the specific astrological case study from the previous page, moving from the general date of the "Year Entrance" (Solar Return) to the precise mathematical verification of the Sun's position. The author is using this case to prove a technical point against the famous 16th-century astronomer Ganesha Daivajña.</meta>

<margin>
Tajika Nilakanthi <note>original: "ता० नी०"; likely referring to the famous 16th-century astrological treatise by Nilakantha.</note>
|| 5 ||
</margin>
<margin>
Chapter 1
|| 5 ||
</margin>

At the time of birth, according to the **Siddhanta Rahasya** <note>A technical astronomical manual used for calculations.</note>, the count of elapsed days <term>Ahargana: the total number of days passed since a specific cosmic epoch</term> is 2128, and the cycle is 6. At sunrise, the **mean Sun** is 9s 6° 7' 43". Added by 22 seconds for the terrestrial longitude <term>Deshantara: a correction for the distance between the birth location and the prime meridian</term>, it becomes 9s 6° 8' 5". When moved by the desired time of 37 **ghatis** and 53 **palas**, the resulting **current Sun** is 9s 6° 45' 25". The anomaly of the Sun <term>Mandakendra: the angular distance of the planet from its apogee</term> is 5s 11° 14' 35". The correction for the center <term>Mandaphala: the equation of the center, used to find the true position from the mean position</term> is positive: 0° 42' 23". The corrected Sun is 9s 7° 37' 48". The precessional degrees <term>Ayanamsha: the correction for the wobbling of the Earth's axis</term> are 17° 49'. The ascensional differences <note>original: "charakhandani"</note> are 75, 60, 25. The positive ascensional correction is 138. Thus, at the moment of birth, the **true Sun** is calculated as 9s 7° 30' 6".

Now, at the time of the **Year Entrance** <note>The Solar Return, when the Sun returns to its exact birth position.</note>, the count of days is 3595 and the cycle is 9. At sunrise, the **mean Sun** is 9s 6° 32' 58". When the terrestrial longitude correction is added, it is 9s 6° 33' 20". When moved by the desired time of 12 **ghatis**, 18 **palas**, and 30 **vipalas**, the resulting **current Sun** is 9s 6° 45' 27". The anomaly of the Sun is 5s 11° 14' 33". The correction for the center is positive: 0° 42' 23". The corrected Sun is 9s 7° 27' 50". The precessional degrees are 18° 26'. The ascensional differences are 75, 60, 25. The positive ascensional correction is 136. Thus, at the moment of the Year Entrance, the **true Sun** is 9s 7° 30' 6".

Because this solar position for the current year has become exactly equal to the Sun's position at the time of birth, the opinion of **Ganesha Daivajña** <note>A renowned Indian astronomer, author of the Grahalaghava (c. 1520 CE).</note> is hereby refuted. It is understood that Ganesha Daivajña did not observe that equality is achieved through this specific method. 

If it is argued that Ganesha Daivajña observed that the Sun in the almanac <term>Panchanga</term>, when moved by the actual hours and minutes, does not reach exact equality in minutes and seconds with the birth Sun—and therefore he proposed an additional correction for the Year Entrance time—we respond by explaining the reason for this discrepancy. The truth is that this difference arises for a reason he did not perceive. We shall demonstrate it: when there are discrepancies between various astronomical systems, such as the **Sauras** or the **Brahmas** <note>Different "schools" of Indian astronomy with slightly different constants for planetary motion.</note>, a gap appears between the Sun at birth and the Sun listed in a standard almanac. 

If both calculations were performed using a single system, no such gap would occur. Furthermore, when calculating the planets for a person's birth, one uses the longitudinal and ascensional corrections for the specific village of birth. However, the creator of an almanac uses the corrections for the village where the almanac is published. Every village has different corrections. This is why a discrepancy arises. If the corrections for the same location were used for both, the gap would never occur. Whichever text was used to calculate the Sun at birth, if the same corrections are applied to the Sun calculated from that same text at the time of the Year Entrance, the values (down to the seconds) will be identical.

<summary>This page provides a detailed mathematical proof showing that the Sun's position at a "Year Entrance" matches the birth position when using consistent astronomical systems. The author uses these calculations to critique the methodology of the famous astronomer Ganesha Daivajña, blaming discrepancies on inconsistent use of local geographic corrections.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical verification of planetary positions at the moment of the Solar Return (Varshapravesha). ...that this is certain. An example of this matter has already been shown previously. Having understood that equality between the two results from this method, the great teacher Shri Keshava Daivajña <note>Keshava Daivajña (c. 15th century) was a famous astronomer and father of the even more famous Ganesha Daivajña. He wrote the Jataka Paddhati, a foundational text for horoscopy.</note>—the crest-jewel of the circle of astrologers—prescribed no other correction even at the time of the **Year Entrance** <term>Varshapravesha: the precise moment the Sun returns to its birth position, marking the start of the astrological year</term>. What was stated by Ganesha Daivajña <note>Ganesha Daivajña (b. 1507) authored the Grahalaghava. Here, the author is siding with the father (Keshava) against the son (Ganesha) on a technical point of correction.</note> is incorrect. || 17 ||

Now, the **true planets** <term>Spashta Grahas: the actual observed positions of planets as opposed to their theoretical mean positions</term> at the time of the Year Entrance must be calculated. For the sake of ease, the author describes the adjustment <note>original: "chalana"</note> to be applied to the planets as listed in the **almanac** <term>Panchanga: the traditional five-attribute Hindu calendar/ephemeris</term>:

Multiply the daily motion of the planet (in minutes and seconds) by the number of days passed or remaining. Divide the result by sixty (60). The resulting degrees, minutes, and seconds should be subtracted from or added to the positions in the almanac. If the interval is "past," subtract it; if it is "remaining," add it. That will be the **true planet** for that specific moment. Note that for planets in **retrograde motion** <term>Vakra: appearing to move backward in the sky</term> and for Rahu and Ketu <note>The North and South Lunar Nodes, which always move "backward" through the zodiac.</note>, the adjustment is known to be the opposite of the other planets.

Example: Within the day and time of the Year Entrance 2 | 12 | 18, the limit...

| 6 | 47 | 0 | 9 | 13 | 48 | 36 | 36 | 36 |

| 17 | 24 | 1 | 23 | 50 | 7 | 11 | 11 | 11 |

Now, the true planets at the time of the Year Entrance are to be determined. For the sake of ease, the adjustment to the planets in the almanac is explained: Multiply the daily motion by the past or future days, then divide by sixty (60). The resulting degrees and minutes are to be subtracted or added; then the planet becomes "true." || 18 ||

Now, the method for deriving the **Moon** from the **lunar mansion** <term>Nakshatra: one of the 27 sectors of the lunar zodiac</term> listed in the almanac is described: Multiply the "past duration" <term>Bhayaata: the time already spent by the Moon in the current Nakshatra</term> by sixty (60), and divide by the "total duration" <term>Bhabhoga: the total time the Moon takes to cross that Nakshatra</term>. This is then combined with sixty (60) times the number of preceding Nakshatras and multiplied by two. The position of the Moon is thus found. The daily motion is found by dividing 48,000 by the total duration of the Nakshatra. || 19 ||

---

...when 6 | 13 | 56 is subtracted from the day and time of the entrance, the "adjustment digits" <note>original: "chalana-anka"</note> are 2 | 58 | 22. All planets have been adjusted using this. || 18 || At the time of the Year Entrance, one must calculate the elapsed **ghatikas** <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes.</note> of the current Nakshatra up to the desired time. These should be multiplied by sixty and divided by the total duration. The result, added to the count of Nakshatras starting from Ashwini <note>The first of the 27 Nakshatras.</note> and multiplied by sixty, gives the total ghatikas from the start of Ashwini to the desired time.

### Tajika-Nilakanthi <note>The signature "Ta. Ni." refers to the *Tajika Nilakanthi*, a seminal 16th-century Sanskrit text on annual horoscopy influenced by Persian astrology (Tajika).</note>

...these result. When divided by nine, the quotient is degrees. Multiply the remainder by sixty and divide by nine to get minutes. Again, multiply the remainder by sixty and divide by nine to get seconds. Thus, the **Moon** <note>original: "shashi"</note> is determined starting from signs and degrees for that specific moment. 

Now, the **Calculation of Lunar Days** <term>Tithisadhana: the method for determining the specific lunar date based on the angular distance between the Sun and Moon</term>. Divide 48,000 <note>This is a mathematical constant used in these specific lunar calculations</note> by the total sum of the past and future duration of the **lunar mansion** <term>Nakshatra: one of the 27 sectors of the lunar zodiac</term>. This gives the **true motion** <term>Spashtagati: the actual velocity of a planet at a specific moment</term> of the Moon in minutes and seconds. 

**Example:** At the time of the Year Entrance, the **Hasta** nakshatra was active. On the previous day, 41 **ghatikas** and 24 **palas** <note>A ghatika is 24 minutes; a pala is 24 seconds</note> had passed. Adding the **desired time** <term>Ishtaghatis: the time elapsed from sunrise to the moment of the Year Entrance</term> of 12 ghatikas and 18 palas, the total time passed within the Hasta nakshatra is 53 ghatikas and 42 palas. Multiply this by sixty (3222). Dividing by the total past and future duration of the nakshatra (58|52), with the dividend 1933|20 and divisor 59|32, the result is 54|44|1. The number of nakshatras from **Ashvini** <note>Ashvini is the first of the 27 nakshatras</note> is 12; multiply this by sixty (720) and add the previous result (54|44|1) to get 774|44|1. Double this: 1549|28|2. Divide by nine: the quotient in degrees is 172|9|47. Divide these degrees by thirty: the result is the **Moon's position in signs** <note>original: "rashyadichandrah"</note> for that moment, which is 5 signs, 22 degrees, and 9 minutes <note>This indicates the Moon is at 22° 09' of the 6th sign, Virgo</note>.

->Now the calculation of the **astrological houses** is stated: || Subtract the desired time of day or night from the half-duration of day or night to find the **hour angle** <term>Nata: the angular distance of a planet from the meridian (noon or midnight)</term>. || 2 || Multiply the **Tropical Sun** <term>Sayanarka: the Sun's position adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</term> by the passed and remaining degrees...<-
->...multiplied by its own rising time and divided by thirty (30), the result should be subtracted from the Sun’s remaining portion. || 21 ||<-

<margin>47</margin> Now, the **Calculation of Velocity** <note>original: "gatyanayanam"</note>. Divide 48,000 by the total past/future nakshatra duration (58|52); the result in minutes etc. is the Moon's true motion, 815|24. In the same manner described, the true planets for that specific moment are established. || 19 ||

Now, in seven verses, the calculation of the **house cusps and junctions** <term>Bhavasandhi: the boundary points where one astrological house ends and the next begins</term> is explained. This includes the calculation of the **Ascendant** <term>Lagna: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth/event</term>, the calculation of the desired time, the **Midheaven** <term>Dashamalagna: the 10th house cusp or the highest point on the ecliptic</term>, and the hour angle. 

First, the knowledge of calculating the **hour angle** <term>Nata-unnata: the distance from the meridian or the horizon</term> is given: If the desired time is before midday, the half-day duration should be reduced by the time passed since sunrise; that is the "East Hour Angle." At night, if the desired time is before midnight, the half-night duration should be reduced by the time passed since sunset; that is the "East Night Hour Angle." 

Regarding the definition: when the desired time is after midday, subtract the half-day duration from the time passed since sunrise; that is the "West Hour Angle." Similarly, after midnight, subtract the half-night duration from the time passed since sunset to find the "West Night Hour Angle." The **Sun for that moment** <note>original: "tatkaliko-arka"</note> must be added to the **precession of the equinoxes** <term>Ayanamsha: the shifting of the Earth's axis which differentiates the Tropical and Sidereal zodiacs</term>. To calculate the Sun's position for the Ascendant, the degrees already passed must be taken.

# Calculation of the Ascendant and Houses

When the calculation of the forward Ascendant <term>Dhana-lagna: a method of calculating the rising sign by moving forward from the Sun's position</term> is performed, the portion of the zodiac sign yet to be traversed (**bhogya**) should be taken. Multiply this by the local rising time of that specific sign (**svodaya**) and divide by thirty. The result—whether it be the Sun’s traversed or remaining portion—should be subtracted or cleared from the **desired time** <term>Ishta-ghati: the time elapsed from sunrise to the moment of the event</term> in **ghatikas** and **palas** <note>1 ghatika = 24 minutes; 1 pala = 24 seconds</note>. 

After that, from the remainder, one should subtract the rising times of the signs yet to come. Specifically: when the "past time" is being calculated, the preceding rising times are subtracted from the multiplied rising time. When the "future time" is being calculated, the subsequent rising times are subtracted. The remainder is then multiplied by thirty <note>original: "kha-agni" (literally 'space-fire' representing 0 and 3, or 30)</note> and divided by the "incomplete" rising time <term>Ashuddhodaya: the rising time of a sign that cannot be fully subtracted from the remaining time</term>. The resulting degrees and minutes should be recorded. This value is then subtracted from a sign that was not fully cleared, or added to a sign that was fully cleared. 

In this way, for both the backward calculation <term>Rina-lagna</term> and the forward calculation <term>Dhana-lagna</term>, based on the count starting from **Aries** <note>original: "Mesha"</note>, the degrees and minutes are adjusted within the sign corresponding to the incomplete rising time. Then, the **precession of the equinoxes** <term>Ayanamsha: the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs, necessary for precise calculations</term> must be applied.

---

From the desired **nadis** <note>another term for ghatika</note> and **palas**, and from the past or future local rising times: [22]
The remainder, multiplied by thirty and divided by the "incomplete" rising time, gives the degrees and minutes.
Subtract or add this to the "incomplete" or "pure" sign, and apply the precession. [23]
Similarly, using the equatorial rising times (**Lankodaya**), the traversed or remaining portions of the Sun are to be subtracted from the time converted to **palas**.
The **tenth house** <term>Dashama-bhava: the Midheaven or the highest point in the sky at the moment of the event</term> is found from the east or west hour angle, just as before. [24]
Adding six signs to the Ascendant gives the **seventh house** <term>Jaya-bhava: the western horizon or descendant</term>; adding six signs to the tenth gives the **fourth house** <term>Turya-bhava: the lowest point or Nadir</term>. [25]
The next six houses are also found by adding six signs to the first six. When a planet's position equals the house center, its influence is full; when it equals the junction (**sandhi**) between houses, its influence is zero. [26]

---

By this method, the **Ascendant** (**Lagna**) <note>The sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> is established. Following this logic, by using the equatorial rising times (**Lankodaya**), the Sun's traversed and remaining portions are calculated. This is subtracted from the time converted to **palas**. The rest of the procedure to find the **tenth house** is the same as described before. 

Specifically: when the hour angle is in the east (**purva-nata**), treat that eastern angle as the desired time, calculate using equatorial rising times, and subtract the Sun's traversed portion; the remaining steps are like the backward calculation. When the hour angle is in the west (**pashchima-nata**), treat that western angle as the desired time, use equatorial rising times, and subtract the Sun's remaining portion; the remaining steps are like the forward calculation. This results in the **tenth house**. 

Having explained the Ascendant and the tenth house, the author now explains how to find the other houses: **By adding six signs** (**sapaddhe**). By adding six signs to the Ascendant, the **seventh house** is found. Similarly, by adding six signs to the tenth house, the **fourth house** is found. Thus, the four cardinal houses are established. Then, subtract the Ascendant from the fourth house; divide the remainder by six to find the "house-sixth" portion. This portion is added to the Ascendant...

Chapter 1
॥ 7 ॥

...should be performed. The junction <term>Sandhi: the boundary point between two astrological houses</term> between the Ascendant and the second house is where the influence of the Ascendant ends and the second house <term>Dhana-bhava: the 'House of Wealth' or second house</term> begins; this should be understood by all. By adding the previously calculated **sixth part** <note>original: "shashtamsha"; the distance between house centers is divided by six to determine boundaries</note>, the first junction is reached. By adding that same sixth part again, the second house center is found. Adding it once more gives the junction between the second and third houses. Adding it again gives the third house center. Adding it again gives the junction between the third and fourth houses. 

In this manner, the first three houses and their junctions are established. Following this, the next three houses and their junctions must be calculated. Specifically: subtract the value of the sixth part from the center. By applying this to the fourth house <term>Sukha-bhava: the 'House of Happiness' or fourth house</term>, the junction between the fourth and fifth houses is found. Adding it again gives the fifth house center. Adding it again gives the junction between the fifth and sixth houses. This results in the sixth house center. Adding it again gives the junction between the sixth and seventh houses. Thus, the first six houses and their junctions are produced. By adding six signs <note>180 degrees</note> to these, the remaining six houses and their junctions are determined. Next, the author explains the purpose of calculating these house junctions:

> By adding the sixth part to the Ascendant, the junction is found; by further additions of the sixth part,
> the three subsequent houses with their junctions are determined. By adding or subtracting the sixth part from the Fourth House, the rest are found. ॥ 26 ॥
> If the traversed or remaining portion of the Sun's sign cannot be cleared from the desired time, multiply the time by thirty (30)
> and divide by the local rising time to find the degrees and minutes. Add or subtract this from the Sun to find the Ascendant; at night, add six signs to the Sun's calculation. ॥ 27 ॥

Whichever house a planet resides in, if its position in signs, degrees, and minutes is exactly equal to the center of that house, then that planet gives its full result—whether auspicious or inauspicious—at a strength of **twenty units** <term>Vimshati-vishvatmaka: a traditional scale where 20/20 represents 100% effectiveness</term>. However, if a planet is positioned exactly at the junction <term>Sandhi</term> between two houses, its result becomes zero. ॥ 20-26 ॥ 

Now, if the **traversed portion** <note>bhukta</note> or **remaining portion** <note>bhogya</note> of the Sun (calculated by the previously mentioned method) cannot be "cleared" <note>subtracted</note> from the **desired time** <term>Ishta-kala: the time elapsed since sunrise</term> in ghatikas and palas, how should the Ascendant be calculated? To answer this, the text says: *If the traversed or remaining portion...* 

If the rising time of the Sun's portion cannot be subtracted from the desired time, then the units of desired time (ghatikas and palas) should be multiplied by thirty and divided by the local rising time <note>svodaya</note> of the sign the Sun is currently in. The resulting degrees and minutes should be noted. These are then added or subtracted from the Sun’s position. Specifically, if you are working with the "traversed" portion, subtract the result from the Sun. If you are working with the "remaining" portion, add the result to the Sun. The resulting position is the **Ascendant** <note>Lagna</note>.

# Calculation of the Night-time Ascendant and the Tenth House

If the calculation of the <term>Lagna: the Ascendant, or the point rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term> is performed for a birth at night, then the calculation should be made from the position of the Sun increased by <term>Rashishatka: six zodiac signs, or 180 degrees</term>. Similarly, when calculating the <term>Dashama-lagna: the Tenth House or Midheaven</term> at night, the "remaining portion" to be calculated should also be based on the Sun's position increased by six signs. Here is an example to illustrate this:

<insert>Rising times at the place of birth</insert>
| Aries | 203 | Pisces |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Taurus | 239 | Aquarius |
| Gemini | 298 | Capricorn |
| Cancer | 348 | Sagittarius |
| Leo | 359 | Scorpio |
| Virgo | 353 | Libra |

<note>These values represent the time in 'palas' (one-sixtieth of a ghati) that each sign takes to rise at the specific latitude of the birthplace.</note>

The rising times at the birthplace are as follows. The <term>Tatkaliko-arka: the "Current Sun," or the precise longitudinal position of the Sun at birth</term> is 9 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds. When the <term>Ayanamsha: the value for the precession of the equinoxes</term> of 18 degrees and 26 minutes is added, it becomes 9s 25° 56' 06". The degrees remaining to be traversed in the current sign are 4° 03' 54". This is multiplied by the rising time of <term>Makara-rashi: the sign of Capricorn</term> (which is 298 in the table) and then divided by 30. This produces the <term>Bhogyakala: the remaining time left for the sign to finish rising</term>, which is 40 minutes, 22 seconds, and 44 thirds.

This value is subtracted from the <term>Ishtanadi: the desired birth time elapsed since sunrise</term>, which is 12 ghatis, 18 minutes, and 30 seconds (converted to 738 minutes and 30 seconds). The remainder is 697:16.

From this remainder, we subtract the rising time of the sign following Capricorn, which is Aquarius (239). The remainder is 458:37:16. Next, we subtract the rising time of Pisces (203). The remainder is 255:37:16. Next, we subtract the rising time of Aries (203). The remainder is 52:37:16. The rising time of the next sign, Taurus (239), cannot be subtracted from the remaining 52:37:16 because it is larger. Therefore, we multiply the remainder by 30 and divide it by the rising time of Taurus (239). The result is 6 degrees, 36 minutes, and 18 seconds.

By adding this to the signs already passed (Aries being the 1st sign), we get 1 sign, 6 degrees, 36 minutes, and 18 seconds. After subtracting the precession (Ayanamsha) of 18° 26', the true Ascendant is found at 0s 18° 10' 16" (Aries). By adding six signs to this, we find the <term>Jayabhavana: the "House of the Wife" or the Seventh House</term> to be at 6s 18° 10' 16" (Libra).

Now, for the example of the Tenth House calculation, we use the <term>Lankodaya: the rising times of the signs at the Equator (Lanka)</term>:

<insert>Table of Equatorial Rising Times</insert>
| Aries | 278 | Pisces |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Taurus | 299 | Aquarius |
| Gemini | 323 | Capricorn |
| Cancer | 323 | Sagittarius |
| Leo | 299 | Scorpio |
| Virgo | 278 | Libra |

The Sun's position is 1s 02° 44'. The time past noon (or birth time) of 12 ghatis, 18 minutes, and 30 seconds is processed to find the "noon-offset," resulting in 0:25:30. This is the "desired time." Using the equatorial rising times, the portion of the sign already traversed by the Sun is multiplied and divided by thirty. The "elapsed rising time" is found to be 279:14:00. This cannot be subtracted from the 25:30 time units, so the 25:30 is multiplied by thirty and divided by the rising time of the sign (323). The result is 2 degrees, 22 minutes, and 6 seconds.

Subtracting this from the Sun's position (9s 07° 30' 06") gives 9s 05° 08' 00". Adding six signs to this gives the <term>Sukhabhavana: the "House of Happiness" or the Fourth House</term> at 3s 05° 08' 00" (Cancer). The difference between the Ascendant and the Fourth House is 2s 16° 54' 44". Dividing this by six gives 0s 12° 49' 37". Adding this value to the Ascendant gives the <term>Viram-sandhi: the junction or boundary</term> of the Ascendant at 11s 00° 59' 53". Adding the <term>Shashtamsha: the sixth part of the house division</term> again gives the start of the Second House at 11s 13° 49' 30". Adding it once more gives the junction of the Second House at 12s 06° 39' 07".

**House Chart**
**Ascendant Chart**

A planet situated between two junctions yields the full result of that house. If it is less than or more than the two junctions, the result belongs to the previous or subsequent house. || 28 ||
The difference between the planet and the junction should be multiplied by twenty (20) and divided by the difference between the house and the junction. The results obtained are known as <term>Vinshopakas: a unit of measure used to quantify the strength or potency of a planet's influence, usually on a scale of 1 to 20</term>. || 29 ||
All the houses thus produced are written together here. || 27 || Alternatively, regarding the results of a planet situated in a house: "A planet situated between two junctions..." as stated. That is to say: if a planet's longitude is greater than the starting junction and less than the closing junction, the result of that house where the planet resides is obtained. If a planet's longitude is less than or greater than the two junctions, the result occurs in the preceding or following house. For example: when a planet is less than the starting junction, it gives the result of the house prior to its current one. When a planet is greater than the closing junction, it gives the result of the following house. || 28 || Now the author describes the house results in terms of <term>Vinshopakas</term>. For example:

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of planetary strengths and house divisions from the previous page. While the previous page calculated the longitudes of the twelve houses and their junctions (sandhis), this page focuses on the mathematical formula for 'Vimsopaka' (a 20-point scale of strength) and provides the 'Drekkāṇa' (Decan) lordship table, which further refines planetary power based on ten-degree subdivisions of the zodiac signs.</meta>

The explanation of the calculation: The Sun is at 9s 07° 30' 06". Its junction <note>original: 'sandhi', the boundary between houses</note> is at 9s 22° 18' 23". The difference between these two is 1s 14° 48' 17". This is multiplied by twenty (20), resulting in 29s 36° 05' 40". The House position is 9s 07° 08' 00" and the junction is 9s 22° 18' 23". Divide the result by their difference of 17:20:23. Now, according to the calculation, the dividend <note>the number to be divided</note> is 1065940 and the divisor is 61823. When the dividend is divided by the divisor, the quotient obtained is known as the **Vimsopakas** <term>Vimsopaka: a traditional unit of planetary strength based on a scale of 20 points</term>, which is 17;14. The same process applies to the Moon and other planets.

Thus ends the results for the houses in the form of Vimsopakas for the Sun and others. || 29 ||

Now, the strength and weakness <note>original: 'balābala'</term> of the planets will be discussed. Since planetary strength cannot be determined without knowing the governing lords, the author first mentions the lords of the signs and the lords of the decans <term>Drekkāṇa: a subdivision of a zodiac sign into three equal parts of 10 degrees each</term>.

Starting with "Mars, etc.": Of **Aries**, the lord is Mars. Of **Taurus**, the lord is Venus <note>original: 'Ushanas'</note>. Of **Gemini**, the lord is Mercury <note>original: 'Saumya'</note>. Of **Cancer**, the lord is the Moon. Of **Leo**, the lord is the Sun <note>original: 'Ina'</note>. Of **Virgo**, the lord is Mercury <note>original: 'Vit'</note>. Of **Libra**, the lord is Venus <note>original: 'Sita'</note>. Of **Scorpio**, the lord is Mars <note>original: 'Ara'</note>.

->The Decan (Drekkāṇa) Chart is written here.<-

| Ausp. | Ar | Ta | Ge | Ca | Le | Vi | Li | Sc | Sa | Cp | Aq | Pi |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **10°** | Ma | Me | Ju | Ve | Sa | Su | Mo | Ma | Me | Ju | Ve | Sa |
| **20°** | Su | Mo | Ma | Me | Ju | Ve | Sa | Su | Mo | Ma | Me | Ju |
| **30°** | Ve | Sa | Su | Mo | Ma | Me | Ju | Ve | Sa | Su | Mo | Ma |

<note>The abbreviations in the table refer to the planetary lords: Ma (Mars/Mangala), Me (Mercury/Budha), Ju (Jupiter/Guru), Ve (Venus/Shukra), Sa (Saturn/Shani), Su (Sun/Surya), Mo (Moon/Chandra). The signs are Aries (Ar), Taurus (Ta), Gemini (Ge), Cancer (Ca), Leo (Le), Virgo (Vi), Libra (Li), Scorpio (Sc), Sagittarius (Sa), Capricorn (Cp), Aquarius (Aq), and Pisces (Pi).</note>

> Mars, Mercury, the Moon, the Sun, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Saturn, and Jupiter are the planetary lords of the signs. The first decans are ruled by Mars and others, the middle ones by the Sun and others, and the third ones by Venus and others, beginning from Aries. These are the lords of the decans. || 30 ||

Of **Sagittarius**, the lord is Jupiter <note>original: 'Ijya'</note>. Of **Capricorn**, the lord is Saturn <note>original: 'Arki'</note>. Of **Aquarius**, the lord is Saturn <note>original: 'Manda'</note>. Of **Pisces**, the lord is Jupiter <note>original: 'Angira'</note>. These planetary lords are the masters of the twelve signs. 

Now, the lords of the decans are explained. Regarding "The first...": a decan is called the third part of a sign, measuring ten degrees. There are three decans in a single sign. Therefore, one should determine which decan a planet occupies: if it is the first, its lordship follows the sequence from Mars; if the second, from the Sun; if the third, from Venus. In this manner, the decan lords for the twelve signs starting from Aries are determined.

Thus ends the Decan Chart. || 30 ||

While the previous page calculated the Vimsopaka strength and decan lordships, this page defines the specific degrees of exaltation and debilitation for each planet and introduces the method for calculating exaltation-based strength (Ucca-bala) and the Nine-fold Division (Navāṃśa) lordships.</meta>

<margin>Tājika Nīlakaṇṭhī || 1 ||</margin>

### The Exaltations and Debilitations of the Planets

Now, the author speaks of the signs and degrees of exaltation and debilitation for the planets. He says, "The Sun, etc." For the planets starting with the Sun, the **exaltation signs** <note>original: 'tuṅga-rkṣa' or 'ucca-rāśi'</note> are as follows: For the **Sun**, it is **Aries** <note>original: 'aja'</note> at the tenth degree <note>original: 'digbhiḥ', literally 'the directions', of which there are ten</note>. For the **Moon**, it is **Taurus** <note>original: 'ukṣa'</note> at the third degree <note>original: 'guṇaiḥ', the three qualities of nature</note>. For **Mars**, it is **Capricorn** <note>original: 'nakra'</note> at twenty-eight degrees <note>original: 'aṣṭa-yamaiḥ', where yama represents 2 and aṣṭa represents 8</note>. For **Mercury**, it is **Virgo** at fifteen degrees <note>original: 'śaraiḥ-ekaiḥ', five and ten</note>. For **Jupiter**, it is **Cancer** <note>original: 'kulīra'</note> at the fifth degree <note>original: 'bhūtaiḥ', the five elements</note>. For **Venus**, it is the final sign, **Pisces** <note>original: 'antya'</note>, at the twenty-seventh degree. For **Saturn**, it is **Libra** at twenty degrees <note>original: 'nakha', the twenty nails of the hands and feet</note>. These should be understood as the points of **maximum exaltation** <term>Parama-ucca: the specific degree where a planet reaches its peak power</term>. || Thus ends the exaltations || 31 ||

Now, the debilitation points of the planets, the calculation of exaltation strength, and the lords of the **Nine-fold divisions** <term>Navāṃśa: a 1/9th subdivision of a zodiac sign, crucial for determining a planet's true strength</term> are discussed. He says, "That [the seventh]..." For those exaltation signs of the Sun and others, the seventh sign—such as Libra for the Sun—is the **debilitation sign** <note>original: 'nīca'</note>. The degrees are the same. For example, for the Sun, Libra at ten degrees is the point of **maximum debilitation**. This logic applies to the Moon and all other planets.

| **Sagittarius** <note>Fire</note> | **Capricorn** <note>Earth</note> | **Aquarius** <note>Air</note> | **Pisces** <note>Water</note> |

This is to be understood for the debilitations. Now, regarding **exaltation strength** <term>Ucca-bala: a measure of how close a planet is to its peak performance point</term>: The planet's position should be subtracted by its debilitation point. If the result is greater than six signs <note>180 degrees</note>, subtract it from the full circle <note>12 signs or 360 degrees</note>. Convert the remainder into degrees and minutes; then divide by nine <note>the divisor for strength units</note> to find the exaltation strength.

Now, the **Nine-fold divisions** <note>Navāṃśas</note> are explained. **Aries** <note>original: 'Kriya'</note>, **Capricorn** <note>original: 'Eṇa'</note>, **Libra** <note>original: 'Tauli'</note>, and **Cancer** <note>original: 'Indubha'</note> are the starting points for the signs in three cycles. To clarify: for the signs Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius, the first Navāṃśa begins with Aries. For Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn, the first begins with Capricorn. For Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius, the first begins with Libra. For Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces, the first begins with Cancer. In this context, the lords of the signs are simply the lords of the Navāṃśas. Thus ends the initial chart. || 32 ||

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Tajika Neelakanthi, a 16th-century Sanskrit treatise on astrology that incorporates significant Perso-Arabic (Tajika) influences. Having established the "Hadda" (Terms) on the previous page, the author now transitions to the calculation of "Panchavargi Bala" or the "Five-fold Strength," which is the core method for quantifying a planet's power in this system.</meta>

<margin>
Tajika Neelakanthi  
|| 10 ||
</margin>

<margin>
Chapter  
1
</margin>

Now, the author speaks of the **Five-fold Strength** <term>Panchavargi Bala: a system in Tajika astrology where a planet's strength is calculated based on five specific zodiacal positions: Sign, Exaltation, Hadda (Term), Drekkana (Decan), and Navamsha (Ninth-part)</term>. He says: **"Thirty in its own sign"** and so on. In its own sign (the sign it rules), a planet has **30** (thirty) units of strength. In its sign of exaltation, it has **20** (twenty) units. In its own term <term>Hadda</term>, it has **15** (fifteen) units. In its own decan <term>Drekkana</term>, it has **10** (ten) units. In its **Musallaha** <note>original: "Musallahe"; here referring to the Navamsha or 1/9th division</note>, it has **5** (five) units. These values are stated by the Great Sages. For the calculation of strength, these are to be considered as **Vishopakas** <term>Vishopaka: a unit of measure in Indian astrology, traditionally representing 1/20th of a full point, used here to quantify planetary power</term> by dividing the final sum by four. || 39 ||

When a planet is not in its own sign, exaltation, term, decan, or ninth-part, the author provides the calculation for other relationships. He says: **"In its various dignities"** and so on. The division of strength described for one's own dignities is modified as follows: In a friend's house, the strength is three-quarters of the original value (reduced by one-quarter). In a neutral's house, it is half. In an enemy's house, it is one-quarter.

To illustrate: for a planet in its own sign, the strength is 30 units. In a friend's sign, it becomes **22;30** (twenty-two and a half). In a neutral sign, it becomes **15** (fifteen). In an enemy's sign, it becomes **7;30** (seven and a half).

Similarly, for the **Terms** (*Hadda*): in its own, it is **15** units; in a friend's, it is **11;15** (eleven and a quarter); in a neutral's, it is

### Thirty in its own sign, twenty in its exaltation; fifteen in its terms, ten in its decans.
### In the Musallaha (Navamsha), five units are declared. These are converted to Vishopakas by dividing by four. || 39 ||
### The strength in one's own dignity is reduced to three-quarters for a friend, half for a neutral, and a quarter for an enemy.
### Sum these equal strengths; when the total is divided by four, a planet with less than five is considered weak. || 40 ||

<margin>|| 10 ||</margin>

seven and a half (**7;30**); and in an enemy's term, it is **3;45** (three and three-quarters).

Likewise, for the **Decans** (*Drekkana*): in its own, it is **10** units; in a friend's, it is **7;30** (seven and a half); in a neutral's, it is **5** (five); and in an enemy's, it is **2;30** (two and a half).

Finally, in the **Ninth-part** (*Navamsha*): in its own, it is **5** units; in a friend's, it is **3;45** (three and three-quarters); in a neutral's, it is **2;30** (two and a half); and in an enemy's, it is **1;15** (one and a quarter).

By determining the five-fold strength in this way and totaling the sum of these five categories, the result—when divided by four <note>original: "Vedo-uddhrute"; Veda represents the number 4</note>—becomes the strength of the planets in **Vishopakas**. A planet with a strength less than five <note>original: "Sharo-nah"; Shara represents the number 5</note> should be known as "weak-strengthed."

As it is said: "One with less than five is of weak power; more than that is called medium. One with more than ten is declared strong; this is known from the Five-fold Strength."

Now, to show a practical example of the sequence, the strengths for Friend, Neutral, and Enemy will be written. **Example:** The Sun is at 9 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds. Its calculation—
